# English–Old English exercise glossary

This glossary supplies English access to the vocabulary and expressions needed for the exercises in all eighteen units: Cook's items, original practice and reading work. The pronunciation practice supplies its own word meanings. English used only to give an instruction, such as “parse” or “give the genitive,” is not vocabulary to translate.

Look up the intended meaning or the whole phrase. Choices under one English word are not always interchangeable. Read the qualifying meaning and case or construction note, then use the forms in the companion Old English–English glossary and the relevant unit. Exercise targets and supplied wording still apply; this reference does not turn supplied material into an independent-recall requirement.

English articles, auxiliaries and dummy subjects often require a construction rather than a matching Old English word. Entries such as **a**, **do**, **will**, **there is**, and phrase entries explain these differences. A case label describes the Old English dependent: “genitive” requires a genitive form, not an extra word meaning “of.”

Old English citation forms retain the book's spelling and length marks. Some source expressions are supplied only in their stated context. Inflected or conventional citation labels are identified where a normal infinitive or full paradigm is not established. Use the separate key to check a completed answer; this glossary is not a replacement key.

## Entries

### A

**a** — Ordinary English a/an usually has no separate Old English article. Ān is used in its taught a-certain-individual sense, not inserted into every indefinite noun phrase. No obligatory indefinite article: bare predicates/nouns often translate a/an. ān is used for the taught introducing-a-certain-individual sense, not inserted everywhere.

**as** — **swā** (adverb; conjunction). Only in sōna swā. as; in the same way as. as; like. Predicative comparison. Swā + adjective/adverb + swā frames equal degree, as … as: the first swā marks degree and the second introduces the standard. **ðæs-ðe** (conj.). Only in supplied ðæs-ðe mē ðyncð. Swā can introduce a predicative comparison, as/like; swā-swā means just as in its supplied comparison. Swilce means as if in the supplied apparent-rejuvenation comparison. Equal degree uses the separate paired swā … swā construction. **ðā** (conj.). Temporal as means when in the expressly supplied factual-past clause with ðā; comparison as uses a different construction.

**be** — Passive weorðan combines with a past participle for the assigned event or becoming. Lexical weorðan become and copular wesan be have separate uses. **wesan** (v.). Copular present/past identification or state; other auxiliary constructions are separate senses. Supplied plural present copula. Copular eom/wæs and bēo indicative series; later passive and compound uses require their own construction. Wesan + the assigned intransitive past participle supplies the taught compound construction. With cumen come it can render have/had come; English have is not an extra word to add to that construction. Passive wesan combines with the assigned past participle; be done includes the participial meaning as well as the finite be auxiliary. The supplied progressive construction is wesan + present participle. Ordinary English be doing can also be rendered by a simple finite Old English verb; this compound is not the universal default.

<!-- entry: EN-926de8edc5ac -->

**be able to** — **cunnan** (v.). know how; be able. Learned skill; plain infinitive complement. Mugan expresses capability, can/be able to, with a plain infinitive. Learned ability can instead use cunnan; neither construction takes an inserted tō before that infinitive. The supplied mugan form expresses capability, can/could/might be able to, with a plain infinitive. It is not the permission sense of English may/might. **mugan** (v.), **cunnan** (v.). Mugan expresses ability or capability. Cunnan + plain infinitive emphasizes learned ability. A modal complement is a plain infinitive, without tō.

**more** — **mā** (adv.). Adverb of amount. more; again. In the negative phrase never more/never again. More in number, distinct from degree or greater value. **māra** (adj.). greater; more important. Value/importance in the life/raiment comparison. more; a greater amount. Nominal/attributive quantity, distinct from more in number. Comparative swīðor expresses rather/more in the preference construction; the positive adverb swīðe means greatly/very. Comparative swīðor expresses more in the degree of an action or feeling, not a count of objects. Mā expresses more in amount or number in its separate uses; māra expresses a greater amount. Comparative swīðor expresses more intensely or preferential rather. Use an agreeing comparative adjective for greater quality/value; these uses are not interchangeable.

**your** — **ēower** (possessive adjective). your; yours. Plural possessors; inflection agrees with the possessed noun. **incer** (possessive adjective). your; yours. Two possessors; agreeing possessive. **ðīn** (possessive adjective). your; yours. One possessor; agrees with the possessed noun. ðīn identifies one possessor and agrees with the possessed noun. ēower and incer mark plural and dual possessors separately. **ðīn** (possessive adjective), **incer** (possessive adjective), **ēower** (possessive adjective). Ðīn is for one second-person possessor, incer for two, ēower for plural possessors. The possessive agrees with the possessed noun.

<!-- entry: EN-d00d825bba6b -->

**yourself** — An ordinary reflexive uses the personal pronoun in the case governed by its verb or preposition. Agreeing self adds the taught emphasis; English -self does not automatically require a separate self. Ordinary reflexive personal pronouns take the governed case; agreeing self adds the taught emphasis. English -self does not automatically require self.

**you two** — Git is nominative dual you two; inc is dative/accusative dual. A dual subject takes a plural verb form.
